Meta’s Muse Charm looks like a Tamagotchi, but it’s tapping into a much newer trend
2026-09-24

Meta’s Muse Charm looks like a Tamagotchi, but it’s tapping into a much newer trend

TechCrunch | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Is Meta’s just-announced Muse Charm a playful device that will make AI more approachable to everyday consumers, or will it be yet another flop in a long list of AI hardware gadgets that didn’t take off, like the Ai Pin, Rabbit, or Friend pendant? Reactions, so far, have been mixed. What the Charm is, however, is on-trend. Like it or not, the form factor could potentially appeal to Gen Z consumers who, in this post-Labubu era, have already been into various dangling items from keychains and bags. It’s all part of a broader kitschy, retro-inspired shift in their approach to fashion and technology. Already, Gen Z has created a market for things that dangle, with everything from lip glosses to hand sanitizers to sunblocks to fragrances and more turned into bag charms, primarily targeted at women. View this post on Instagram A post shared by Kristina Rodulfo (@kristinarodulfo) A post shared by Kristina Rodulfo (@kristinarodulfo) Hailey Bieber’s viral Rhode lip case, which stuck the lip gloss onto a phone case, had a big influence on this idea that beauty products could also be fashion accessories, as did its other beauty-focused bag charms. (The brand saw $212 million in annual net sales from a lineup of roughly 10 products, Vogue noted last year, as the company announced its billion-dollar exit to Elf Beauty.) Then there was the Labubu craze, which saw sales of the so-ugly-it’s-cute fuzzy monster bag charm explode, growing Pop Mart’s (the Chinese retailer behind the product) revenue to $1.8 billion in 2024, a 7x jump from 2019. While demand for the Labubu itself has since faded, the concept of the decorative bag charm has not. One analyst firm estimates the global bag charm market will grow to over $1 billion by 2030. Today, bag charms can be characters, bedazzled beauty products, or other whimsical designs or objects, like Coach’s coveted cherry charms, a Gen Z favorite. They’ve proven popular because they’re a means of expressing your personality, just as humans have done for ages through their fashion choices, makeup, and jewelry. Similarly, Meta’s Muse is a customizable character; you design your own avatar, which makes the AI agent a reflection of you, too. And, as many have pointed out, Muse Charm’s form factor recalls a Tamagotchi, the pocket-sized digital virtual pet that became a major toy fad in the late 90s and again in the mid-2000s. However, the Tamagotchi is not the most current reference. It seems the Muse Charm is actually a twist on what some young people are already doing with their Apple Watch. Often meant as a way to repurpose an older device, the concept of the Apple Watch keychain or charm has been growing in popularity, fueled by TikTok videos showing different ways to use the watch when not on your wrist. never even used it for years! About to use it everyday now 🤓 #applewatch #applewatchhacks #applewatchtips #apple #lvwallet On retailers like Amazon and Walmart, and marketplaces like eBay and Etsy, there are thousands of Apple Watch keychains, straps, pendants, lanyards, clips, and bag charm cases now available in nearly every style you can think of. As you might expect, these keychain watches are often being adopted by younger women as a fashion accessory first, rather than a piece of technology. I’m obsessed 💕 #applewatch #teacher #teachersoftiktok #girly #cute This all then intersects with the broader retro-tech trend, which has seen older tech like digital cameras, flip phones, iPods, CDs, cassette tapes, home phones, and wired earbuds making a quiet comeback. This vibe shift in tech comes as young people describe the addictive, algorithm-driven feeds and “soulness” nature of technology as making them feel more disconnected, which has also fed into this trend of wanting tech they can touch and feel. (One startup, Clicks, is even bringing back the physical phone keyboard in a BlackBerry-inspired device, for instance.) For Gen Z, these physical objects help them feel more connected with the technology they’re using and offer a sense of control over a world where technology has become so deeply integrated into our lives that it no longer feels like a choice to use it. Young women, again, have been at the forefront of this trend, with some even crafting their own portable, mini computers, “cyberdecks,” which they fit inside clamshell purses or custom cases, and bedazzle with jewels, flowers, stickers, pearls, and other items to make them feel more personal. That brings us back to the Muse Charm. While it’s clearly designed with the tech-as-fashion-accessory trend in mind, that doesn’t mean it will be a hit. Meta has abused consumer trust over the years to such an extent that it may never fully recover. To name just a few examples: The tech giant had to settle with the FTC over charges that it deceived consumers by making users’ private information public without their consent, and has been penalized by the FTC for numerous privacy-related violations. In 2023, the FTC charged Meta with violating a privacy order that was filed after the 2019 settlement. It’s also been repeatedly hauled before Congress over its harms to minors, has lost or settled multiple lawsuits over the same topics, and has seen various whistleblowers outing its abuses. These careless privacy practices could ultimately see Meta lose the battle to be the consumer AI of choice… unless, of course, consumers decide that the “devil you know” is better than the one you don’t. (Especially when the latter are those warning AI could put an end to the human race.) Meta’s end game, after all, is highly-targeted, highly-personalized ads. If that’s the privacy tradeoff in gaining access to free AI — Meta said it will monetize Muse by taking a small fee from transactions — consumers could be willing to take the risk. Databricks buys Row Zero and is scouting for more startups to acquire
2026-09-24

Databricks buys Row Zero and is scouting for more startups to acquire

TechCrunch | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Databricks announced on Thursday that it has acquired an early-stage spreadsheet startup called Row Zero. The story goes that Databricks’ finance team fell in love with Row Zero because it was a cloud tool that could scale beyond 1 million live spreadsheet rows. They were combining Row Zero with Databricks’ homegrown AI agent called Genie. With Genie, Databricks users can use natural-language prompts to ask complicated business questions of their data stored in Databricks systems, such as analyzing profit margins or the status of sales prospects. When the executive team asked the finance team about this cloud tool they were using, a light bulb went off. “Spreadsheets are one such interface that every business analyst loves. So it makes a lot of sense to intermarry Business Intelligence (BI), Agents, and Spreadsheets!” Databricks CEO Ali Ghodsi posted on X about how the acquisition went down. So an enterprise’s secure data lives in Databricks’ cloud storage, and AI agents and humans interact with it through the spreadsheet format and formulas they know well, rather than using and learning some other business intelligence software. By integrating Row Zero with Databricks, the data can be manipulated in a spreadsheet form but remain secure, rather than transferred to an insecure spreadsheet that is shared around. Terms of the deal were not disclosed. Row Zero, founded by former AWS and Tableau engineers, raised $10 million in May 2025, at an estimated $40 million valuation at the time, according to PitchBook. Databricks, which closed another $5 billion in funding in August and hit $7 billion in annualized revenue run rate, has been busy shopping in 2026 and is eager to keep doing so. “We intend to do many more acquisitions like this in the future,” Ghodsi told TechCrunch. In March, Databricks bought two startups, Quotient AI, which does evaluation and reinforcement learning for AI agents, and very early-stage interactive notebook SiftD.ai. In June, the company bought AI security operations center startup Panther, previously valued at $1.4 billion in 2021. Last month, it bought Electric, makers of a small, lite Postgres database known as PGlite, useful for agents running locally on devices. No More ‘Trump Avenue’: Canada’s Capital Moves to Rename Street Amid Tense Trade War
2026-09-24

No More ‘Trump Avenue’: Canada’s Capital Moves to Rename Street Amid Tense Trade War

TIME | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings U.S. President Donald Trump speaks with Canada's Prime Minister Mark Carney at the G7 summit, in Evian, eastern France, on June 16, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Ottawa's City Council voted unanimously on Wednesday to remove President Donald Trump’s name from a residential street in the Canadian capital amid an increasingly hostile trade war. The motion to rename “Trump Avenue” in the city’s Central Park neighborhood was brought forward by River Ward councillor Riley Brockington on Sept. 9 and swiftly received support from other local officials. “I don't like the idea of anything that's named for Donald Trump, who's demonstrated hostility towards Canada,” Ottawa Mayor Mark Sutcliffe said at a news conference after the vote. The move symbolizes the strained relations between the two countries, as Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney goes head-to-head with the Trump Administration in a trade standoff. Canada’s retaliatory tariffs came into effect on Sept. 8, matching the economic pressure imposed by the U.S. after negotiations collapsed last month. Trump’s repeated threats to annex Canada and his insistence on referring to it as the “51st state” have significantly contributed to the tensions. The streets in Ottawa’s Central Park neighborhood were named after New York City institutions in the late 1990s and early 2000s, when Trump was primarily known for his businesses and real estate ventures, long before his political aspirations came to fruition. Brockington said a consultation with local residents will now follow, with alternative names proposed for “Trump Avenue” ahead of a vote. “I could speak at great length why I believe, strongly, it is time to rename ‘Trump Avenue,’ but I don't want to give a platform anymore to the person who is on the current street sign blades,” Brockington said. “I do believe this is about a new beginning, and I want this to be a community-led process where the community ultimately will select and recommend to City Council what that new name will be.” In a statement to TIME, White House spokesperson Anna Kelly referred to the name change as a “meaningless gesture” and said “these local governments should do right by the citizens they serve, thank President Trump for defending their country, and make a good deal to benefit Canadian and American citizens alike.” TIME has reached out to Ottawa's City Council for comment. The vote to cut the street name comes after President Trump recently pushed through controversial renaming efforts of his own. In late August, he signed an Executive Order renaming Lake Ontario, which borders the Canadian province of Ontario and the U.S. state of New York, as “Lake America.” The order applies to U.S. federal references of the lake and does not determine how Canada—or others—refer to the body of water. Several lawmakers across both countries have rejected the new moniker. Thomas Juneau, a professor of public and international affairs at the University of Ottawa, says the street name change in Ottawa is “illustrative of the mood” of Canadians. “Polls have repeatedly [shown] how extremely unpopular Trump is, and how his targeting of Canada on trade, and his trolling on social media, have made him and the U.S. unpopular,” he tells TIME. A poll by Politico, conducted Sept. 13-15, found that 83% of Canadians thought it was wrong for Trump to impose tariffs on their country, while 71% backed Carney’s retaliatory tariffs. Another poll, conducted by Abacus Data between Sept. 4-9, found that 82% of Canadians had a negative impression of Trump. The 'Trump Avenue' street sign is seen in a suburb of Ottawa on Jan. 26, 2021. Michel Comte––Getty Images In a new interview, Carney admitted he had prepared over the past 12 months or so for the “extreme tail risk” that Trump might order military action against Canada, amid the U.S. President’s repeated mentions of his desire to annex the country. “I think you have a responsibility in these roles to look at extreme tail risk,” Carney told the New York Times. “That’s just risk management. That’s not a base case, but it would be irresponsible not to” prepare. Juneau says the “risk of American military intervention against Canada is extremely low,” but reasons that just because Canada “shouldn't take [Trump’s] threat literally, it doesn't mean they shouldn't take it seriously.” In an April 2025 interview with TIME, Trump denied he was “trolling” when discussing annexation. “We’re taking care of their military. We're taking care of every aspect of their lives… We don't need anything from Canada. And I say the only way this thing really works is for Canada to become a state,” he argued. Carney, in line with other Canadian officials, has vehemently pushed back against the idea, stressing that Canada is “not for sale.” In June, after Trump revived talk of his ambitions to annex Canada, Ontario Premier Doug Ford simply stated “Canada will never be the 51st state.” Despite the tensions, Carney said he and Trump have spoken “frequently” since trade talks collapsed last month. However, there is no clear path toward an end to the trade standoff. “Trump is actively trying to choke off the Canadian economy," says Juneau, noting that a key sticking point in trade negotiations, according to Carney, has been Washington's efforts to restrict Canada’s ability to negotiate trade deals abroad. The U.S. accounted for 72.5% of Canadian merchandise exports in 2025, according to the country’s annual trade report, making it Canada's largest trading partner by a wide margin. Canada and the U.S. are also deeply integrated through defense arrangements, including their membership in NATO and the North American Aerospace Defence Command (NORAD)—a bilateral defense and national security arrangement involving the shared defense of the continent. Amid the strained relations with the Trump Administration, Carney has sought to diversify Canada’s trade and security alliances to reduce its reliance on the U.S. “The weaponization of that interdependence by the U.S. makes [Canada] extremely vulnerable in a way that pretty much nobody else is,” insists Juneau, pointing to the urgency of Carney’s task. The Canadian government has set itself  a goal is to “double non-U.S. exports in the next decade” as it seeks to expand trade with other markets. In January, Carney met Chinese President Xi Jinping to forge what he called a “new strategic partnership” to end Canada's economic reliance on the American market. Canada is also currently in negotiations with India as it seeks to negotiate a new comprehensive economic partnership agreement with Delhi, and is in talks for a free trade agreement with the Philippines. The diversification efforts extend into defensive measures. At the E.U.-Canada summit in June 2025, Canada established a new Security and Defense Partnership with the bloc. Canada later became the first non-European country to participate in the "Security Action for Europe” (SAFE) instrument, which provides low-interest loans to help member states purchase military and defense equipment. Canada is seeking to further strengthen ties overseas by becoming the European Union’s first “associate member”—a goal European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen has vowed to help make a reality. The proposed arrangement, the details of which have yet to be ironed out, would likely deepen cooperation on matters including trade, defense, AI, and Arctic security. Carney made his pledge to E.U. lawmakers when he addressed the European Parliament in Strasbourg, France, on Sept. 17. Touting closer ties with the bloc, he said “Europe and Canada are stronger together” and insisted "we are not fair-weather allies, we believe that our ​prosperity grows when ⁠it is shared." During Carney’s trip to Europe, Canada applied to join the British-led Joint Expeditionary Force military coalition—a force launched in 2014 and made up of 10 North Atlantic NATO member states that conduct rapid-response and security operations. Juneau warns that despite Carney’s current push to diversify trade and defense away from the U.S., the results of these deals may take a long time to truly stand up, as "in practice, diversifying trade and security relationships is measured in years and decades."

The 5 Best Strength Exercises for People Over 50
2026-09-24

The 5 Best Strength Exercises for People Over 50

TIME | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Some changes famously accompany aging. For some people, it gets harder to hear in crowded places; for others, reading a menu in dim light gets trickier. But not everyone knows to expect a loss of muscle mass. Starting around age 30, people lose 3-5% of their lean muscle per decade. For most people, this decline isn’t noticeable until around age 60, when it might start to make daily activities more difficult. One of the most effective ways to combat muscle loss is to build and protect the muscle you already have through strength training. Not only will that help you avoid frailty and falls, but it can also keep you moving with ease through your daily activities. When people ask Charlie Grundas, a certified personal trainer and owner of We, The Collective Fitness in Chicago, why it’s important to strength train after 50, he has an easy reply: “Do you ever want there to be a time where you can’t get off the toilet?” he says. “I don't think there should ever be [a time when] you drop something on the floor, and you're like, ‘Well, forget it, I don't own that anymore.’” You also start losing bone density as you age, and strength training builds healthy bones, which helps prevent falls and fractures. Plus, the muscle you build may offer some insulating protection if you do stumble, says Will Hewitt, certified personal trainer and director of training at Flight House Fitness in Charlestown, Mass. Strength training can help at any age, whether you want to keep improving in your athletic pursuits, be able to pick up grandchildren, or simply bring your groceries inside in one trip, Grundas says. It’s OK to start with just a few exercises and just a few sets and repetitions: anywhere from one to three sets of six to 15 repetitions is beneficial, according to the American College of Sports Medicine. What’s most important is that you do it—ideally at least twice a week. It’s always a good idea to get the green light from your doctor before starting any new exercise routine. You might also consider working with a fitness professional, like a certified personal trainer or certified strength and conditioning specialist, if you’re new to strength training at this stage of life or if you’ve had any injuries or surgery. A professional can help you modify movements and swap out equipment that might feel better for your particular circumstances, Hewitt says. They can also help encourage you to try activities you might think you “shouldn’t” do in your 50s and beyond. “Your days of doing an 18-inch box jump may be over, and that's a hard pill to swallow, but let's do a six-inch box, and maybe six months from now, we'll be doing 12 inches,” he says. “It's just like eating your vegetables: You just have to keep doing it, and you'll be better for it.” Here are some of the best strength-training exercises he and others recommend you try as you get older, plus how to do them with proper form. A squat is the movement required to stand up from sitting down, which is something you’ll want to be able to do your whole life long, Grundas says. This particular variation works your quads, hamstrings, glutes, and core, and it’s approachable for people of all fitness levels, Hewitt says. Holding a weight at your chest can help you keep your chest up, and keeping a wide stance can help you squat more deeply, according to the National Academy of Sports Medicine. Start standing with your feet slightly wider than shoulder-width apart, toes pointing slightly out, holding a dumbbell (cupping one end like it’s a heavy goblet) or kettlebell at your chest with both hands. Push your hips back and bend your knees to lower your body until your thighs are parallel to the floor or as far as you can comfortably go, keeping the weight close to your chest and your elbows inside your knees. Press through your feet to return to the starting position. Complete all repetitions, aiming for six to 15. You can work all the same muscles by doing a step-down instead. Standing on a stair, start to walk down, and as soon as your heel touches the lower step, press through your standing leg and return both feet to the higher stair, Grundas says. Repeat, alternating feet with each repetition. At the end of the weekly yoga class he teaches, Grundas challenges his class to stand up from the floor without using their hands. This works many different lower-body muscles and your core, in addition to helping you maintain your mobility and your balance. A 2026 European Journal of Preventive Cardiology study found people who could sit down and stand up from the floor without using their hands or other body parts for support had a four times lower risk of death over the study period compared to people who required more support. Sit down cross-legged on the floor using as little support from your hands or legs as possible. Stand up, once again using as little support as possible. Complete all repetitions, aiming for three to five. Use your hands or legs as needed to help you sit and stand. Keep practicing, aiming to be able to do the movement with less help. If you’re able to sit and rise without support, challenge yourself with a Turkish get-up instead, Grundas says. This movement can help you maintain your ability to put dishes away on the highest shelf in your kitchen or stow your suitcase in the overhead compartment on a plane, Grundas says. Start sitting on a bench or a sturdy piece of furniture with your feet planted on the floor, holding a dumbbell in each hand at your shoulders with your elbows bent and your palms facing forward. Press both dumbbells overhead until your arms are straight but not locked. Pause briefly, then lower the dumbbells to return to the starting position. Complete all repetitions, aiming for six to 15. If you need something a little easier, use light weights to do a lateral raise, Grundas suggests: Start standing with your feet shoulder-width apart, holding a dumbbell in each hand by your sides, palms facing your body. Keeping your arms straight and your shoulders back and down, slowly lift the dumbbells up and out to your sides until the dumbbells are level with your shoulders. Slowly lower the dumbbells back to the starting position. Complete all repetitions, aiming for six to 15. If you’re ready for a more challenging move, try a bench press, he says: Lie on your back on a flat bench with your feet flat on the floor holding a barbell with your hands slightly wider than shoulder-width apart. Press the bar up until your arms are straight, but don’t lock your elbows. Slowly lower the bar with control until you’re just about at your chest. Complete all repetitions, aiming for six to 15. Grundas recommends a modified deadlift to help you maintain your ability to pick something up off the ground. Stand on one foot with the toes of your other foot on the floor slightly behind your heel, like you’re in a staggered “kickstand” position. Keep most of your weight in your standing leg and a slight bend in that knee. Hold a dumbbell or kettlebell in your opposite hand. Keeping that slight bend in your standing knee, push your hips back and lower the weight in front of your leg until you feel a stretch in the back of your thigh. Pause briefly, then press through your standing foot to return to the starting position. Complete all repetitions, aiming for six to 15, then repeat on the other side. Try it without any weight at first to get used to the movement. Once you feel comfortable in the kickstand position, you can try a single-leg Romanian deadlift, where you extend your kickstand leg straight behind you instead. You can also lift heavier weight with both feet on the floor in the traditional Romanian deadlift position. Core exercises help improve balance, which can ward off falls, Hewitt says. Your core muscles also include your pelvic floor, so strengthening these muscles can help prevent bladder and bowel issues that become more common with age. Start on your hands and knees on the floor. Press through your palms and toes to lift your knees a few inches off of the floor. Keep your back flat and hips level as you hold this position for 10 to 60 seconds. Elevate your hands on a sturdy bench, step, or couch to make this a little easier. Once you get comfortable holding this position, make it a little more challenging by lifting one hand off the floor at a time and tapping it to the opposite shoulder or holding one arm and the opposite leg in the air for several seconds, Hewitt says. You can also crawl forward and backward in this position without letting your knees touch the floor.

Republican Panic Sets In Over Prospects of a Massive Blue Wave

Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Sen. Ted Cruz of Texas waves to supporters with Rep. Ashley Hinson of Iowa, who is in a high-profile race for Senate against Democratic state Rep. Josh Turek, during a fundraiser in Clive, Iowa, on August 29, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Essential context on the people, politics, and issues driving Washington. By signing up you are agreeing to our Terms of Service and Privacy Policy. With less than six weeks to go until Election Day, the sentiment among Republicans is shifting from nervous to fatalistic, as a pileup of bad news has many convinced they are heading toward an electoral disaster. The warning signs are coming in quickly from across the map—much on it from red states where President Trump won comfortably just two years ago. Outside groups backing the GOP are now spending in the millions on Senate races in once-safe territories like Nebraska and Kansas; South Carolina, Mississippi, South Dakota, and Arkansas are all drawing renewed attention amid fears of a historically massive blue wave. At a closed-door briefing on Wednesday with his caucus, Republican Sen. Tim Scott of South Carolina delivered a particularly grim message: 2026 is not going to look anything like 2024. As leader of the National Republican Senatorial Committee, Senate Republicans’ official campaign arm, Scott is tasked with deciding how to dole out a sizable warchest in the final weeks of the midterms. To wit, he told his fellow Republican Senators that the NRSC’s top priorities are now almost entirely defense, as they scramble to avoid losing GOP-held seats in Texas, Iowa, Maine, and Alaska. They are also still investing to flip Democratic seats in Michigan and New Hampshire—where some recent polls show Democrats Abdul El-Sayed and Chris Pappas with small leads over Republicans Mike Rogers and John E. Sununu. Notably absent from this plan were North Carolina and Georgia—two Senate seats Republicans were touting as major targets last year. Now some are writing those off as all but won by Democrats Roy Cooper and Jon Ossoff. An operative familiar with Scott’s thinking stressed that the NRSC’s strategy remains fluid until Election Day and noted that outside groups could still step in to back up their candidates in those states. A day earlier in Iowa, which Trump won in 2024 by 13 points, state lawmakers received an even starker warning. Radio host Steve Deace, a real player in conservative circles there and a loyal Trump ally, told his followers on Wednesday afternoon that “Iowa House Republicans were informed by their own pollsters that if you are in a +15 district you’re within the margin for error, if you’re in a +10 district you’re underwater, and that this might end being an even worse midterm environment than 2006.” Iowa Republicans on the ballot are not being subtle about their efforts to put some distance between their fortunes and the MAGA Movement. Rep. Zach Nunn voted last week to curb Trump’s deeply unpopular war against Iran—a month after campaigning with Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, who is running that war. Rep. Ashley Hinson, the GOP nominee for the open Senate seat, is running ads touting her work with Democrats, not her endorsement from Trump. As Deace noted, “If the environment is this bad in my home state, where Trump has been the most popular political figure over the last decade, I can’t imagine where it is everywhere else.” There’s similar scrambling in Texas, where a super PAC blessed by Senate Majority Leader John Thune is now on track to spend more than $100 million, an add on of $35 million to the $65 million the group had already booked in advertising to boost Ken Paxton’s bid against James Talarico. The total price tag for keeping the Senate seat is on track to approach $200 million just on the GOP side. It’s little better for House Republicans. Between their official campaign arm, allied outside organizations, and the candidates themselves, there is a massive $485 million well of ad reservations between Aug. 1 and Election Day—and that’s just in districts Trump won, according to a Politico analysis. Even Speaker Mike Johnson has grown frustrated with Trump and his constant introduction of tumult in the political ether. During his Sept. 13 appearance on NBC’s Meet the Press, Johnson appeared obviously annoyed as he kept getting asked about Trump’s fresh promise to send every American adult a $5,000 check if Republicans keep their House and Senate majorities. “I guess we’re going to spend the whole segment talking about this one issue,” Johnson said. “I want to talk about what we’ve actually done and not ideas on the table.” It’s a common frustration across the party as gas prices—diesel especially—seem to know no limits, a war in Iran grinds toward its eighth month, and Russia’s war with Ukraine continues despite Trump’s promises to end it on “day one.” With all those drags on their ticket, Republicans have struggled to impress upon voters bragging points like the lowest crime rate in 126 years and declining prescription drug costs, or passing a massive tax cut. The dynamic has left Republican strategists deeply frustrated. They have plenty of cash, succeeded (for the most part) in recruiting solid candidates, and are facing an opposition party that has spent much of the year fighting its own coalitions. Yet the environment still seems to be trending bluer than anyone anticipated. A simmering fear is that the trendlines may get even worse in the coming weeks. As one Republican strategist told me this week, Election Day cannot come quickly enough.

AI Arrived at the Worst Possible Moment
2026-09-24

AI Arrived at the Worst Possible Moment

TIME | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings AI is not arriving in a world capable of receiving it. Instead, it is coming into a world that has spent the last two decades systematically dismantling the very foundations that would allow us to deploy such a powerful technology responsibly. Institutional authority, shared truth, and information integrity have been battered. We are building the most sophisticated cognitive technology in human history atop the most fractured trust landscape in modern memory. If you wanted to design the worst possible moment for AI to emerge, you might create a moment like this one. A decade ago, we worried about living in a “post-truth” world. Oxford Dictionaries selected “post-truth” as its Word of the Year in 2016, defining it as circumstances in which objective facts are less influential in shaping public opinion than appeals to emotion and personal belief. Post-truth describes a world where facts still exist but are often overshadowed by emotional narratives. What we are experiencing now goes deeper. I call this the “post-trust era:” a world where the very mechanisms that allow us to establish trust have broken down. It is not just that emotions sometimes trump facts. It is that we have lost shared foundations for deciding what counts as a fact in the first place. Look at what has happened to our information ecosystem over the past two decades. We moved from a world in which truth was curated by editors, printed by presses, and distributed by a small number of recognizable intermediaries, to a world in which anyone can publish, amplify, distort, or fabricate. Data exploded, and social media fragmented audiences. Algorithms began optimizing for clicks, engagement, and sensationalism. The incentives shifted from accuracy to attention: virality over veracity, outrage over nuance, speed over scrutiny. The numbers tell their own story. Nearly 8 in 10 people get directed to their news primarily through algorithmic systems such as social feeds, search engines, and aggregators, effectively outsourcing their information diet to systems they neither understand nor fully trust. In the United States, trust in the federal government has fallen from over 70% in the late 1950s to under 20% today. Confidence in mass media has dropped: fewer than one-third of Americans express even a “fair amount” of trust. Religious institutions, financial systems, healthcare organizations, and even science itself have seen trust erode. Post-truth describes a distortion in how people weigh facts and feelings. Post-trust describes a deeper fracture: the loss of shared procedures for determining what is real. When the first photographs appeared in the 1830s, people marveled at their fidelity. For nearly two centuries, seeing was believing. A picture was not perfect, but it was evidence. In early 2023, an AI-generated image of Pope Francis wearing a stylish white puffer jacket spread across social media platforms. It was compelling because it sat in a narrow band between familiar and unexpected. The photo’s style, posture, and lighting borrowed credibility from decades of real photography. Many accepted it as real before they had time to question it. Within two years, AI-generated images, voices, and video had become so pervasive and so convincing that the question was no longer “can you spot the fake?” but “can you trust anything you see?” The ability to fabricate convincing “evidence” at scale has arrived just as our information ecosystem is most fragmented. Research from Duke and NYU shows that exposing people to opposing political views on social media can increase polarization rather than reduce it. A landmark study in Science found that false news spreads significantly faster and more widely than trustworthy news online. Neuroimaging research suggests that when people encounter information challenging their political beliefs, brain regions associated with negative emotion light up, while confirmatory information activates reward pathways. We are not only divided about facts. We are wired to process confirming and disconfirming information through different cognitive channels altogether. That is the substrate into which AI arrives: a world where information is abundant, but trust is scarce, where consensus reality has splintered, and where the mechanisms for establishing shared truth have broken down. Into this fractured substrate arrives something genuinely new. Not just another tool, but the first technology in history whose core competence is cognition. Past technologies extended our capabilities while remaining subordinate to human thought. The printing press amplified the words we chose to print. Electricity transformed the energy we decided to harness. Combustion engines replaced the muscle power we used, and computers executed calculations we programmed. The internet distributed the messages we wrote. AI breaks that pattern. It no longer merely executes tasks. It generates arguments, synthesizes information, makes recommendations, and produces creative work that feels, at a human level, like someone else’s thoughts. A single model can write an essay, summarize a legal transcript, translate a medical note, draft code, plan a marketing campaign, and compose music. At the heart of most of these systems is an architecture that learns by predicting what comes next, a word, pixel, or token, in a sequence. These models do not know “truth” in the way humans understand it. They discover patterns, frequencies, and correlations in whatever data they are given. And here is where the timing becomes critical: the data they are given is generated in a post-trust world. AI is trained on our news stories, our social media feeds, our digitized books, our biased archives, our polarized debates, our conspiracy theories, our scientific breakthroughs, and our misinformation. It absorbs not only our knowledge but our distortions. It learns not only our facts but also our conflicts about those facts. The result is a strange mirror. AI reflects us to ourselves, refracted through statistical patterns and scaled by computation. It gives us answers we cannot easily audit, based on training data we cannot thoroughly inspect, optimized for objectives we do not always control. A new kind of cognitive infrastructure is emerging at the precise moment our social infrastructure for trust is under strain. Excerpted from The Trust Code with permission from Tiffany Xingyu Wang. What the World Owes the Marshall Islands
2026-09-24

What the World Owes the Marshall Islands

TIME | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings This week, the U.N. General Assembly affirmed that statehood endures the loss of land. My country, the Republic of the Marshall Islands, will persist despite rising seas. The question is how Marshallese people go on living here safely. Our nation consists of narrow strips of land on 29 low-lying coral atolls scattered across more than 750,000 square miles of the central Pacific Ocean. For many, adapting to rising seas may mean moving communities to higher ground. In the Marshalls, we have no higher ground to move to. That fundamental difference shapes what climate adaptation means for us and how we must act. As we prepare for another El Niño, our people know its rhythms well, but the severity with which these forces now arrive is worsening. First, the rains come heavily. Then they stop. Water tanks empty, wells run dry, crops fail, and ocean conditions shift. We begin shipping water to communities with none. These changes are the reality behind the historic diplomatic achievement taking place this week. The U.N. General Assembly will very likely adopt the first Sea-Level Rise Declaration by consensus. In a year of fractured diplomacy, that is worth celebrating. The Republic of the Marshall Islands has never wavered in its position. Sea level rise will not alter the statehood, sovereignty, or maritime zones of any state. But while the legal status, rights, and maritime zones of my country and its people are now secure, our physical safety is not. That distinction must guide what comes next. The world must respond to the impact of sea level rise on coastal communities, heritage, and cultures, and the lands that have nourished our ancestors for generations. We must start by preventing as much sea level rise as we can. Island nations have long fought for climate action. The Alliance of Small Island States, the High Ambition Coalition (which the Marshall Islands convenes), and other progressive forces fought hard for the 1.5°C temperature limit within the Paris Agreement. And last year, inspired by a campaign led by Pacific youth, the International Court of Justice affirmed in its advisory opinion on climate change that every country's emissions reductions must align with 1.5°C. The Court also affirmed that the continued licensing, subsidy, production, and consumption of fossil fuels may constitute internationally wrongful acts. Yet earlier this month, the United Nations Environment Programme released a report indicating that an overshoot of 1.5°C is now almost certain. Crossing the threshold cannot be an excuse to abandon the target. Every fraction of a degree means greater risks, higher seas, and more lives and livelihoods lost. Our responsibility is to make that overshoot as small and short-lived as possible. In the Pacific, we know that fossil fuel dependency hurts not only the planet but also our health, security, and economies. That is why, earlier this month, Pacific leaders set out our vision for a fossil fuel-free Pacific. If the world does not follow science, international law, or the Pacific by phasing out fossil fuels as swiftly as possible, the harm to the roughly one billion people who live on the world’s coasts will be devastating. What’s more, the costs of adaptation will rise exponentially. For the Marshallese people, the desire to stay in the land of our ancestors is deeply felt. We demand action from major emitters as we also adapt to rising seas. Adaptation is a huge technical challenge. We are mapping our atolls from the air, meter by meter, because available satellite elevation data has a margin of error nearly three times the height of most of our nation. We cannot determine which land to raise or whose drinking water can survive a drought from inaccurate data. Precise information is also essential to develop credible proposals and access climate finance. We know what practical adaptation looks like. Our National Adaptation Plan is complete; funding it is not. Effective adaptation costs money—$310 billion to $365 billion per year to manage worsening climate impacts—which is why the call in the Declaration on Sea Level Rise to streamline and simplify access to finance matters. Pacific leaders in Palau put it plainly: what this region needs is climate finance, and finance that actually reaches people on the ground. We need states to invest in helping communities adapt, protecting livelihoods, allowing people to remain safely on their lands for as long as possible, and ensuring that those who move can do so with dignity. The Marshall Islands is proud to lead the champions launching the Rising Nations Institute, which aims to translate the principles we have fought for into practical solutions for communities confronting rising seas. Despite geopolitical challenges, UNGA’s Sea-Level Rise Declaration shows that the world is committed to climate action and to the protection of countries threatened by sea level rise. We now have legal frameworks capable of enduring physical change and inspiring action. The next challenge is to ensure that our systems of finance, planning, and adaptation can do the same. We cannot completely hold back the rising seas. But we can do a lot more to limit that rise and to choose how we meet it. 7 Fall Yard Habits That Help Pollinators Survive Winter
2026-09-24

7 Fall Yard Habits That Help Pollinators Survive Winter

TIME | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A few years ago, Katie Costanzo and her students went curbside shopping for trash. They collected eight bags of leaves that homeowners around Buffalo had raked up for disposal, then sifted through them to see what—if anything—was hiding inside. The bags were anything but empty. They contained insects from more than 25 families, representing every stage of life from eggs and larvae to pupae and adults. There were bees, wasps, lacewings, hoverflies, beetles, and more—all tucked into material their human landlords had deemed garbage. The class exercise was later published in American Entomologist. That’s the problem with what we call “fall cleanup.” Costanzo, an ecologist and entomologist at Canisius University, wishes people would stop viewing leaves, stems, and wood as trash. “This is part of the ecology of our system,” she says. “This could be habitat or resources.” Much of what looks dead, messy, or finished for the season is still serving as food, shelter, or a nursery. And leaves are only the beginning. Queen bumblebees may be tucked just beneath the soil in your yard. Native bees and ladybugs can shelter inside hollow stems you might otherwise have snipped and stuffed into a yard-waste bag. Beetles, flies, and other insects hide in spent flower heads, while birds return to those same plants for seeds. Once cold weather arrives, a yard might look like it’s closed for the season, but “all this business is happening” through the winter, says Mary Phillips, head of native plant habitat strategy at the National Wildlife Federation. Pollinators are animals that move pollen from one flower to another, allowing plants to reproduce. In an American yard, that group includes native bees, butterflies, moths, beetles, flies, and some wasps—not only the honeybees most people picture. The same habitat also supports plenty of beneficial creatures that aren’t technically pollinators, including predators and decomposers that help keep an ecosystem functioning. Kim Eierman, an ecological landscape designer and author of The Pollinator Victory Garden, encourages homeowners to think differently about even the smallest patch of land. “You have an ecosystem,” she says. “It may be a really tiny one. Maybe you’re lucky and have a lot of land—but you have an ecosystem, so we need to start treating our landscapes that way.” That means reconsidering some familiar features, including the closely cropped lawn she calls a “green desert.” Here are seven fall habits pollinator experts practice in their own yards—and how to make each one manageable. Before you pick up a rake, divide your yard mentally into zones. Continue maintaining the high-traffic areas where kids play, pets run, or people gather, as well as walkways and other spots that need to remain clear. Then identify one quieter space—under a tree, behind shrubs, alongside the house, or at the back of a garden bed—where leaves, stems, and a few branches can remain. Costanzo does this in her own yard. “You don’t have to manage your entire property the same,” she says. Her family keeps recreational areas usable and designates other spots for pollinator gardens and small piles of natural material. Someone with a suburban lot might preserve only a few sticks and one bed of leaves. That still counts. “Anything—any change—is good change,” she says. If you’re worried that neighbors will mistake habitat for neglect, put up a sign explaining what you’re doing. “I have a pollinator sign. I have a native-plant sign,” Eierman says. “It’s a clue to neighbors and passersby that we’re gardening a little differently—and we’re gardening with a purpose.” The same zoning strategy applies to the lawn: “I start to cut back—not cut the lawn back, but cut back on how often I mow,” Phillips says of the turf her family still uses. As growth slows in the fall, she spaces out mowing and leaves the grass a little taller. A patch set aside as habitat is different: Once insects have settled in, leave it undisturbed through the winter rather than mowing it down. Eierman would like to rebrand “leaf litter” as “nature’s gold.” Fallen leaves insulate bumblebee queens near the soil surface where they hibernate and shelter butterflies, moths, caterpillars, and many other invertebrates. As the leaves break down, they also return nutrients to the soil, suppress weeds, and become free mulch and compost. “Nature knows what it’s doing,” Costanzo says. “We don’t have to clean it up.” The most practical approach isn’t necessarily to leave every leaf exactly where it lands. The National Wildlife Federation recommends moving leaves off turf, pavement, driveways, and other places where a thick or slippery layer would create problems. Rake them beneath trees or into garden beds, aiming for a layer about 3 to 5 inches deep. If you still have extras, pile them in a little-used corner and let them slowly become leaf mold. Lightly spray newly placed leaves with a hose to keep them from blowing away on a windy day. If you plan to shred fallen leaves with a lawn mower and leave the pieces on the grass, do it early in the season, before many insects have settled in for winter, Eierman says. “If you leave all those leaves and then start chopping them up, you’re chopping up lots of invertebrates, too,” she says. When you send occupied leaves to the curb, “you’re throwing away a part of your ecosystem,” Costanzo adds. There are exceptions: Diseased plant material and invasive plants may need to be removed; ask a local extension office how to dispose of them. Leaf litter can harbor ticks, so keep it away from play spaces and heavily used paths, and follow local public-health guidance. In wildfire-prone regions, habitat advice never outranks defensible-space rules. For example, CAL FIRE advises removing leaves and other combustible debris from the first 5 ft. around structures and maintaining additional cleared zones beyond that. Regional rules vary, so check with your local fire authority. When an aster, ironweed, Joe-Pye weed, or native grass turns brown, resist the urge to shear it to the ground. Hollow and pithy stems can serve as nesting or overwintering sites; flower heads shelter small beetles, flies, and weevils; and seed heads become winter buffets for birds. Eierman has watched goldfinches descend on the plants in her yard to pry out seeds. “They look dead to you,” she says. But they haven’t clocked out yet. The easiest option is to leave most plants standing through the winter. Phillips’s Joe-Pye weed grows so high that they’re “taller than me,” she says. To keep those lanky stalks from toppling, she gathers them with twine, transforming them into an architectural feature rather than a botanical pileup. You can shorten especially unruly stems, but don’t cut them all the way down: Leave at least a foot standing. Eierman trims only some hollow or pithy stems and varies their heights, creating openings for stem-nesting bees of different sizes. Leave plenty untouched, too. The point is variation—not precision pruning with a ruler. Diseased plants are the exception. Remove affected material rather than preserving it alongside healthy stems, and use local extension guidance to determine whether it should be trashed, burned where legal, or processed another way. Normal browning at the end of the season isn’t itself a disease. Bee housing doesn’t always resemble a charming wooden hotel. Many native bees nest in the ground, burrowing a few inches into exposed soil and spending much of their lives there. A landscape sealed beneath thick wood-chip mulch offers them fewer entrances. Look for a quiet spot where you can pull back the mulch and leave a modest patch of soil exposed and undisturbed. It doesn’t need to dominate the garden. Even small openings between plants can help, especially when they aren’t covered with plastic sheeting, landscape fabric, or another barrier. The Xerces Society offers guidance on recognizing and preserving ground-nesting habitat. If you like mulch because it suppresses weeds and looks polished, keep using it where you need it; just don’t smother every square inch. “Everybody’s situation is different,” Costanzo says. The goal is to create access, not a moonscape. A fallen limb doesn’t automatically need to leave the property. Decaying logs, sticks, and small brush piles provide structure and shelter for insects and other wildlife; birds also forage there for food. “Keep some of the sticks, keep some of the larger pieces of wood,” Costanzo says. “It really does provide structure and habitat that’s so important in the winter.” That doesn’t mean every branch must remain exactly where it lands. “You don’t leave them where they fall necessarily,” Phillips says. She moves dead branches, logs, and brush into a corner of her yard—like behind shrubs, where they’re less conspicuous. “Sometimes a pile like that can be really helpful,” she says. Start small: Place a log along the back of a garden bed, tuck a few sticks beneath a shrub, or build one restrained brush pile in a low-traffic area. Keep dead wood away from the house, and don’t leave a damaged tree standing if it could fall on a person, building, power line, or neighboring property. The National Wildlife Federation has additional guidance on dead wood and wildlife habitat. Fall isn’t only a season for leaving things alone. In many parts of the country, it’s an excellent time to plant: Cooler temperatures can reduce stress on new plants, and nurseries may discount perennials that look tired but have perfectly viable roots. “Here in the Northeast, our optimal times for planting are September, October,” Eierman says. “That’s what I’m doing right now.” The ideal window varies, so check local recommendations and leave enough time for roots to establish before the ground freezes. Start with one or two species rather than redesigning the entire property by sundown. Choose plants native to your region and suited to the site’s sunlight, soil, and moisture. Costanzo’s family has added everything from oaks, dogwoods, and redbuds to Joe-Pye weed and black-eyed Susans. “We’ve seen such a difference,” she says. “We have so much diversity.” Last year, seven bird species nested on their property. Some plants pack an especially powerful ecological punch. “The oak tree is the superstar,” Phillips says: Across the U.S., oaks support hundreds of species of butterfly and moth caterpillars. If you don’t have room for a giant tree, native shrubs, grasses, and flowering perennials still count. Enter your ZIP code into the National Wildlife Federation’s Native Plant Finder to see locally useful options. The right list will look very different in Pennsylvania than it does in Arizona or Oregon. Native plants can be pricey, but fall bargain-hunting may pay off. Phillips looks for end-of-season markdowns at nurseries that carry them. “They may not look good, to be honest,” she says. You might put one in the ground and immediately cut it back—but “then they’ll come back the next year.” When shopping, ask whether a cultivated variety still produces pollen, nectar, and seed. “You don’t want sterile plants,” Eierman says. “You want plants that actually produce seed.” Some cultivars provide nectar for pollinators but no seeds for birds, making them less useful than they first appear. Fall is also seed-collecting season. Gather mature seeds from native plants you already grow and spread them elsewhere on your property. Phillips does this with plants she wants to expand into other parts of her yard. Some seeds, including those of many milkweeds native to cold-winter regions, need a period of low temperatures before they germinate. Plant them in fall, Phillips says, and winter will handle the chilling for you. Treat the problem, not the calendar. Pesticides and herbicides should answer a specific need—not arrive automatically with the turning leaves. Broad-spectrum insecticides don’t distinguish between a mosquito and the many insects you want to keep. Costanzo recommends using them “in response to something rather than proactively,” and only when truly necessary. “They kill every insect,” she says. “They’re not specific.” Herbicides create a different problem: They eliminate clover, violets, dandelions, and other plants that can provide food for insects. “Your lawn doesn’t have to look like a carpet,” Costanzo says. Keep turf where you need it for recreation, but consider allowing more plant diversity elsewhere. That doesn’t mean never treating a genuine infestation or health threat. Identify the problem first, then use the most targeted approach available rather than spraying preventively across the entire property. Follow the label exactly. The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency recommends using pesticides only when necessary and treating only the specific area that needs treatment. Also reconsider the assumption that every nibble is damage. A chewed leaf can be evidence that a native plant is feeding the ecosystem exactly as intended. When Costanzo notices signs of hungry insects, she reminds herself: “I’m supporting the ecosystem. I’m so glad these insects are finding a resource here.” A few holes usually won’t kill a healthy plant, she adds—and flawless foliage isn’t much use to a caterpillar. These changes benefit the gardener, too. In an August 2026 National Wildlife Federation survey of more than 1,000 wildlife gardeners, 96% said the practice had improved their mental health, and 93% reported physical-health benefits. Respondents most often described the joy of seeing wildlife, a lift in mood, and the satisfaction of knowing they were making the world a little better. Unlike many long-term environmental problems, the payoff can appear surprisingly quickly. “Within a season, you’re seeing the wildlife come,” Phillips says. “You’re seeing the butterflies. You’re seeing the birds; you’re hearing the birdsong.” For Eierman, that ability to take action—and see it rewarded—is part of the appeal. “There are very few things we have control over in our lives—particularly the ones that give us a lot of pleasure,” she says. “This is one. The effort that you put into it comes back to you in a big way.”

News and Politics - Slate Magazine
What Are Progressives Supposed to Do About Public Education?
2026-09-24

What Are Progressives Supposed to Do About Public Education?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-24

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Over the weekend, the New York Times Magazine published a piece by journalist Nikole Hannah-Jones, in which she grapples with the decision to send her daughter to a low-income majority-Black poorly rated public school in her Brooklyn, New York, neighborhood. Hannah-Jones has made her career writing about race, class, and school segregation, and a decade ago she wrote about the choice to forgo the quote-unquote better school options those in her educational and economic cohort generally chose. “Saying my child deserved access to ‘good’ public schools felt like implying that children in ‘bad’ schools deserved the schools they got, too,” she wrote back in 2016. “I understood that so much of school segregation is structural—a result of decades of housing discrimination, of political calculations and the machinations of policy makers, of simple inertia. But I also believed that it is the choices of individual parents that uphold the system, and I was determined not to do what I’d seen so many others do when their values about integration collided with the reality of where to send their own children to school.” Ten years later, her daughter is 16, and old enough to have a say. And for high school, she made about as different a choice as possible, opting into one that is highly rated and selective—and majority white and Asian. And as she’s realized just how behind she is at her new school, and all she hadn’t learned at her previous ones, she flat-out told her mom that she feels betrayed. For elementary school and middle school, Hannah-Jones’ daughter, Najya, went to schools where the lunchroom was a chaotic scrum, hallway fights happened often, teachers spent an enormous amount of time on disruptive students to the neglect of those who were there to learn, homework wasn’t assigned, in-class assignments weren’t corrected, and even though she got A’s in math, it turns out she couldn’t do basic algebra or even long division by high school. When she made the decision to put her daughter in a troubled school, Hannah-Jones wrote that “I also knew that we would be able to make up for Najya anything the school was lacking.” That turned out not to be the case. Intensive math tutoring seems to have gotten her up to speed. But that came after failing a state math exam, something Hannah-Jones wrote off as her daughter simply struggling with standardized tests. And it came with tanking confidence and growing anxiety from a kid who is clearly bright, curious, and highly capable. The response to Hannah-Jones’ update has been overwhelming. Many people (myself included) applauded her for her honesty and her ability to look quite critically at how her own choices—made in accordance with strongly held and admirable principles—may have harmed the one person in the world she’s supposed to most ferociously protect. Others picked apart her parenting choices, mocked the very principles that informed her decisions, or turned the problems in some public-school classrooms into ones caused by their own pet theories. There were grosser reactions, which I suspect stem from some conservatives’ continued obsessions with battling another Hannah-Jones endeavor, the 1619 Project, that are well worth ignoring entirely. At its core, though, Hannah-Jones’ article is as much about the shameful realities of too many American public schools (especially those that serve Black and brown students) as it is much more fundamental questions: What is the purpose of public education? What do we owe everyone’s children? And what specific obligations do we owe to our own? These questions were political fault lines well before this story published. To many conservatives, this seems obvious: You have obligations only to your own child, what you owe anyone else’s is minimal, and your own authority over your children is ultimate and nearly unlimited. The state, in this view, shouldn’t have much of a role in educating children in the first place—that is the job of the parent (never mind that more than a quarter of American adults are nearly or functionally illiterate and 15 percent cannot do the most basic math, such as adding or subtracting). When it comes to what you owe your child, well, that should also be up to the parent. Pro-family conservatives routinely oppose basic child protection laws, like having social workers check up on homeschooling families or bar people convicted of crimes against children from homeschooling. They have fought efforts to ban child marriage and to criminalize the practice of teachers hitting or otherwise abusing children in the classroom (including disabled students). Empowered within the Trump administration, they have set about dismantling the Department of Education and America’s long, proud, and astoundingly economically fruitful tradition of publicly educating all of its residents. Progressives and liberals see it differently, to put things mildly. Foundational to both worldviews is the idea that citizens should have equal rights and opportunities. Public education serves that purpose: Without it, individual opportunity waxes to extremes and wanes down to near-nothing according to the incomes of one’s parents. Public education is also broadly socially beneficial: The more educated the populace, the better able it is to participate in our democratic processes and to contribute to the economy. The abilities to logically reason, to analyze, and to work through complex problems are skills built by literacy that also contribute to a more productive and peaceful polity. When kids from less-fortunate families have access to a good education, they have the chance to pull themselves up and bring subsequent generations along with them. These ideals, though, have crashed into the hard reality of American life and our country’s history. Equal opportunity has never really existed, especially for Black people, in America. The truth of our history is that an entire population was brought here and forced into slavery, their families separated, their rights zero. They were barred from learning how to read, let alone from getting to vote or even being considered full citizens. That was followed by decades of legally enforced segregation and discrimination and policies that are hard to see as anything but either intentionally or negligently impoverishing. While the era of legal segregation in schools and elsewhere has ended, segregation itself has not. American public schools, including those in minority-white New York City, remain sharply segregated along racial and economic lines. The question of how to fix this is one that has roiled the broader left for decades, and that animated Hannah-Jones’ initial decisions about her daughter’s education. In one telling, Hannah-Jones seemed to believe that her obligation to everyone’s children and to the public school system generally was more important than any obligation to her own child. There are many progressives who make this argument: One idea is more or less that no child is a special snowflake with more rights than any other, and so in the interests of equity, you should curtail your privileged child’s opportunities. (“They’ll be fine” is a typical refrain here.) Another, more compelling argument is that if enough privileged parents send their kids to “bad” schools, then eventually the “bad” schools will improve, and that these are basic social obligations we owe one another. (A less-convincing corollary is that the “bad” schools aren’t bad at all, it’s just racist parents who believe that some schools are bad—an argument starkly contradicted by Hannah-Jones’ experience, as well as things like test scores, which, to be fair, many detractors also say are racially biased.) The problem, though, is that “Sacrifice your child’s education because they are not special and other kids need more” is not exactly a compelling sell to many parents. This is a broader problem with many progressive efforts: Without the ability to immediately overhaul all of society, making outcomes more equal turns instead into a race to the bottom. But everyone being equally poorly off isn’t particularly appealing to most people. It also runs counter to most parents’ impulses, which are to protect their children and to expand their universes of opportunity. This is of course not true for all parents, which is also an inconvenient truth that both conservatives and progressives seem to sidestep. Some parents are truly awful: abusive, neglectful, don’t care if their child gets a decent education or becomes a violent hellion who makes life for his teachers and classmates hell. Many more are simply incapable of good parenting, so caught up in their own struggles they cannot give their children even the basics. Many conservatives are eager to heap scorn on and blame these parents, but then their answer for what to do to help their children is a shrug—or, worse: expel or jail the most troubled kids, which can wreak generational havoc. Too many progressives, on the other hand, refuse to acknowledge that many of the problems plaguing public schools do in fact stem from dysfunctional family situations and checked-out educators, and that that reality—not just bigotry—might shape the willingness of more-engaged families to send their children to institutions where dysfunction is the norm and learning is limited. In truth, most parents will put their own child’s needs first, even if they say they don’t believe their child should be treated better than any other. You buy your kid a coat; you may donate one to a homeless shelter, but you probably aren’t buying coats for every kid in America (or every kid the world over). This is a good thing. Efforts to sever a parent’s particular and deep ties to their own offspring run counter to the basic biological imperatives that enable the continuity of the human race, and routinely end in tragedy. But it’s also intolerable to live in a society that forgoes its obligations to other people in it—especially children, who are any society’s smallest, most malleable, and most vulnerable members, and those who will shape its future. These two things—obligations to one’s own child, obligations to everyone’s children—can sometimes be in tension. But they don’t have to be in total conflict if we adopt a general politics of understanding that the things you want for your child are probably similar to the things other children need, too—and if you can see advocating for your child and advocating for the needs of others as mutually reinforcing rather than a zero-sum game. Education is a good example. American public schools were not bestowed upon us by God; we can change how we fund them, how we staff them, and what we expect from them. We can also take a view that I suspect is held by Hannah-Jones, that a good education means more than algebra and literacy (although it obviously has to mean those things, too). A good education also means experiencing the friction and the sparks of interacting with people who are different from you—whose families look different from your own, whose assumptions and priors do not match yours, who have more resources than you, who have less. A good education also does start at home—and that means raising your children to know that you, the parent, have their back and are their ultimate and unwavering advocate, and also that you’re modeling how to be a decent person in the world, which means caring about other people, too. What does this mean in the real world? It won’t be the same for every family. At the very least, I would argue that it means supporting evidence-based and appropriately funded public education that rejects the dogmas and ideologies du jour in favor of rigorous academics, high expectations, and ample support to help students achieve what they can. It means not lowering the bar just because some students have a harder time clearing it, but figuring out how to give those students the help they need to get over it. One mistake progressives have made in the past few years is seeing differences in outcome as evidence of a fatal flaw in whatever is measuring those outcomes (standardized tests, for example), rather than as weather vanes pointing toward what needs more attention. The removal of test scores in college admissions and the growing hostility toward putting high-achieving kids into gifted programs feed into this sense that progressive education policy is more about taking away than building up—that the promise of equality isn’t about lifting up the less fortunate, but tearing down those who are doing well. Appropriately balancing your obligations often means giving particular consideration to your own child, who is, after all, here only because you chose to bring them into the world. Making schools and society better does not have to start with making things for your child worse. It does mean, though, figuring out what “better” means. Are rankings and test scores the be-all and end-all to what you would consider a good education? Or do you care as much about your child being curious even if there’s no professional or rational economic purpose behind it? How much value do you put behind your child learning how to navigate a diversity of experiences and interactions with many different kinds of people? With the right political choices, we can have all of the above. But we’re not going to get there if people on the left start from a place of presumed scarcity, demanding that individual parents make intolerable sacrifices that set back the people they love most, and if the progressive message is that the only way to even the scales is by setting both sides to zero.
